I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two arrays `a` and `b` of the same length `n`
2. We can perform one transformation on array `a`:
   - Choose `k` distinct indices (0 ≤ k ≤ n)
   - Add 1 to each of the selected elements
   - Permute the resulting array in any order
3. We want to know if we can transform array `a` into array `b` using exactly one such transformation

## Key Insights

1. After adding 1 to some elements, we can reorder the array arbitrarily
2. This means we only care about the frequency of values, not their positions
3. For the transformation to work:
   - We need to add 1 to some elements of `a` to get values that match `b`
   - Since we can permute freely, we need to check if there's a valid way to "add 1" to some elements of `a` to match the multiset of `b`

## Analysis

Let's think about what happens:
- Original array `a` has values with certain frequencies
- After adding 1 to some elements, we get a new array where some elements are increased by 1
- Since we can permute, the resulting values in `b` should be achievable by taking values from `a` and adding 1 to some of them

So the algorithm:
1. Count the frequency of each value in array `a`
2. Count the frequency of each value in array `b`
3. For each value `x` in `b`, we need to check if it comes from a value `x-1` in `a` (by adding 1) or if it's from a value `x` in `a` (unchanged)

More specifically:
- For each value `v` in `b`, if `v` appears `count_b[v]` times, then:
  - Some of these `count_b[v]` elements must come from elements in `a` that had value `v-1` (after adding 1)
  - The remaining must come from elements in `a` that had value `v` (unchanged)

4. We need to ensure that for each value `v`:
   - `count_b[v] ≤ count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]`
   - And for values where `v-1` is not in the original array, we only have elements from `v`

Actually, let's think more systematically:

For each value `v` in the target array `b`:
- The elements with value `v` must come from either:
  - Elements in `a` that originally had value `v` (remaining unchanged)
  - Elements in `a` that originally had value `v-1` (after adding 1)

So the constraint is: `count_b[v] ≤ count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]`

But we need to make sure this works for all values simultaneously. Actually, we don't need to worry about the direction - we just need to check if we can "cover" all elements of `b` using elements from `a`.

Wait, let me reconsider: We add 1 to some elements, then permute. So every element in `b` must be obtainable from an element in `a` either:
- By adding 1 to an element that originally had value `x`, resulting in `x+1`
- Or by not changing an element that originally had value `x`

So for each value `v` in `b`, it comes from either:
- An element that originally had value `v` (unchanged)
- An element that originally had value `v-1` (after adding 1)

Therefore, the total count of `v` in `b` must be less than or equal to the sum of:
- Count of `v` in original `a` 
- Count of `v-1` in original `a`

But we also need to ensure we don't overuse the elements. Each element in `a` can only be used once.

The constraint is: `count_b[v] ≤ count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]` for all values `v`.

Additionally, we need to ensure that the total "increase" we make makes sense - but actually, this constraint should capture the full picture.

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1**: `a = [-1, 1, 0]`, `b = [0, 0, 2]`
- `count_a[-1] = 1, count_a[0] = 1, count_a[1] = 1`
- `count_b[0] = 2, count_b[2] = 1`

Check:
- For v=0: `count_b[0] = 2 ≤ count_a[0] + count_a[-1] = 1 + 1 = 2` ✓
- For v=1: `count_b[1] = 0 ≤ count_a[1] + count_a[0] = 1 + 1 = 2` ✓  
- For v=2: `count_b[2] = 1 ≤ count_a[2] + count_a[1] = 0 + 1 = 1` ✓

Yes, this works.

**Example 2**: `a = [0]`, `b = [2]`
- `count_a[0] = 1`
- `count_b[2] = 1`

Check:
- For v=2: `count_b[2] = 1 ≤ count_a[2] + count_a[1] = 0 + 0 = 0` ✗

This fails, so answer is "NO".

**Example 3**: `a = [1,2,3,4,5]`, `b = [1,2,3,4,5]`
- `count_a[1] = 1, count_a[2] = 1, count_a[3] = 1, count_a[4] = 1, count_a[5] = 1`
- `count_b[1] = 1, count_b[2] = 1, count_b[3] = 1, count_b[4] = 1, count_b[5] = 1`

Check:
- For each v: `count_b[v] ≤ count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]`
- Since count_a[v] = 1 and count_a[v-1] is 0 for v=1 (v-1=0 not present), we have 1 ≤ 1 + 0 = 1 ✓

This works, so answer is "YES".

Wait, for v=1: count_a[1] = 1, count_a[0] = 0, so 1 ≤ 1 + 0 = 1 ✓
For v=2: 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓ (count_a[2] + count_a[1] = 1 + 1)
Actually, I think I made a mistake in the frequency setup.

For v=2: we need 1 element in b with value 2, which can come from:
- Elements with value 2 in a (count_a[2] = 1)
- Elements with value 1 in a (count_a[1] = 1)

So yes, 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓

The constraint `count_b[v] ≤ count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]` should work for all v.

But what about negative values? We need to handle them properly.

Let me code this with proper frequency counting.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Count frequency of each value in array `a`
3. Count frequency of each value in array `b`
4. For each value `v` that appears in `b`, check if `count_b[v] <= count_a[v] + count_a[v-1]`
5. If all values satisfy this condition, return "YES", else "NO"

Note: For v-1 that doesn't exist, count_a[v-1] = 0.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count frequencies
    count_a = {}
    count_b = {}
    
    for val in a:
        count_a[val] = count_a.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    for val in b:
        count_b[val] = count_b.get(val, 0) + 1
    
    possible = True
    # Check all values that appear in b
    for v in count_b:
        # Count of v in b must be <= count of v in a + count of (v-1) in a
        if count_b[v] > count_a.get(v, 0) + count_a.get(v-1, 0):
            possible = False
            break
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two arrays `a` and `b` of the same length `n`
2. We can perform exactly one transformation on array `a`:
   - Choose any non-negative integer `k` (0 ≤ k ≤ n)
   - Choose `k` distinct indices and add 1 to each of those elements
   - Then permute the array in any order
3. We need to check if we can transform array `a` into array `b` using exactly one such transformation.

## Key Insights

1. Since we can permute the array after the addition, the order doesn't matter - we only care about the multiset of values.

2. The transformation adds 1 to exactly `k` elements of array `a` (where `k` can be 0).

3. After adding 1 to `k` elements and permuting, we get array `b`.

4. This means that if we sort both arrays, the difference between corresponding elements in the sorted arrays should be consistent.

5. Specifically, if we sort array `a` and array `b`, then we should be able to "explain" the difference between them by adding 1 to exactly `k` elements.

6. Since we can only add 1 (not subtract), the values in `b` should be at least as large as the corresponding values in the sorted `a`.

7. For each element in the sorted `b`, it comes from some element in the sorted `a` that was increased by some amount.

8. The key insight: if we sort both arrays, then the difference between sorted `b` and sorted `a` should have exactly `k` elements where the difference is 1, and the rest should have difference 0.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we can only add 1 to elements, so the difference between corresponding elements in sorted `b` and sorted `a` should be either 0 or 1, and the number of elements with difference 1 should be at most `n`.

But more precisely: we add 1 to exactly `k` positions (we can choose which positions), then we permute. So after sorting, we can match the elements optimally.

The correct approach:
- Sort both arrays
- For each position i, `b[i] - a[i]` should be either 0 or 1
- The number of positions where `b[i] - a[i] == 1` should be exactly `k` (which can be any value from 0 to n)
- Since we can permute after the addition, we should be able to match the elements optimally - so the differences should be non-negative and at most 1

Actually, since we can only add 1, `b[i]` must be equal to `a[j]` for some j, where `a[j]` was increased by 1 if j was selected, or remains the same.

The key insight: if we sort both arrays, then `b[i] - a[i]` should be either 0 or 1 for all i, and the sum of `(b[i] - a[i])` should equal `k` (the number of positions we increased).

Therefore, for sorted arrays, we need:
- `b[i] >= a[i]` for all i
- `b[i] - a[i]` is either 0 or 1 for all i
- The sum of `(b[i] - a[i])` is some k where 0 ≤ k ≤ n

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: a = [-1, 1, 0], b = [0, 0, 2]
- Sorted a: [-1, 0, 1]
- Sorted b: [0, 0, 2]
- Differences: [0-(-1)=1, 0-0=0, 2-1=1] = [1, 0, 1]
- All differences are 0 or 1 ✓
- Sum of differences = 2, which is between 0 and 3 ✓
- YES

Example 2: a = [0], b = [2]
- Sorted a: [0]
- Sorted b: [2]
- Difference: 2-0 = 2
- Difference is 2, which is not 0 or 1 ✗
- NO

Example 3: a = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], b = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
- Sorted a: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
- Sorted b: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
- Differences: [0, 0, 0, 0, 0]
- All differences are 0 ✓
- Sum of differences = 0 ✓
- YES

This matches the expected output!

## Algorithm

1. For each test case:
   - Read n, array a, array b
   - Sort both arrays
   - For each position i from 0 to n-1:
     - If b[i] < a[i], return "NO"
     - If b[i] - a[i] > 1, return "NO"
   - Return "YES"

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort both arrays
    a.sort()
    b.sort()
    
    # Check if we can transform a to b
    possible = True
    for i in range(n):
        if b[i] < a[i]:
            possible = False
            break
        if b[i] - a[i] > 1:
            possible = False
            break
    
    print("YES" if possible else "NO")
```

Add $1$ to each of $a_{i_1}, a_{i_2}, \ldots, a_{i_k}$, all other elements of array $a$ remain unchanged.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two arrays `a` and `b` of the same length `n`
2. We can perform exactly one transformation on array `a`:
   - Choose `k` distinct indices (0 ≤ k ≤ n)
   - Add 1 to each of the chosen elements
   - Then permute the elements in any order
3. We need to determine if we can transform array `a` into array `b` using exactly one such transformation.

## Key Insights

1. After the transformation, we can reorder elements freely, so the multiset (or frequency distribution) of elements matters.
2. The transformation adds exactly `k` ones to `k` different positions in array `a`.
3. Since we can permute freely, we only care about the frequency counts of values in both arrays.

## Analysis

Let's think about what happens:
- Original array `a` has some counts of each value
- After adding 1 to `k` elements, we effectively increase `k` values by 1
- This means some values in the array will be increased by 1

So if we sort both arrays and compare their frequency distributions, we should be able to see if `b` can be obtained from `a` by adding 1 to some elements.

However, since we can permute freely, we just need to check if we can "match" the elements by increasing some of them by 1.

## Approach

1. Count the frequency of each element in array `a`
2. Count the frequency of each element in array `b`
3. For each value in `b`, check if it can come from a value in `a` by adding 1
4. Specifically, for each value `x` in `b`, we can get `x` from `x-1` in `a` (if `x-1` exists)
5. The constraints are:
   - The "original" values in `a` must have been transformed to match `b`
   - We can only add 1, not subtract
   - We need to ensure the transformation works

Actually, let's think differently:

- For each value `v` in array `b`, it must come from some value `v'` in array `a` where `v' ≤ v`
- If `v' = v`, then it's unchanged
- If `v' = v-1`, then it was increased by 1

So we can think of it as: we have a frequency distribution of values in `a`, and we want to get to the frequency distribution of `b`.

The transformation adds 1 to exactly `k` elements. This means:
- Some elements remain the same
- Some elements increase by 1

Therefore, we can model this as:
- Let `freq_a[x]` be frequency of value `x` in array `a`
- Let `freq_b[x]` be frequency of value `x` in array `b`

For each value `v` in `b`:
- It can come from `v` in `a` (unchanged)
- Or it can come from `v-1` in `a` (increased by 1)

So we need to find a valid assignment such that:
- All elements in `b` are covered
- The total count of "increased" elements is some `k` (0 ≤ k ≤ n)

But since we can only add 1, we can only go from `x` to `x+1`.

Therefore, the frequency constraint must satisfy:
- `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]` for all `x`
- Actually, more precisely: each value `x` in `b` can come from either `x` in `a` or `x-1` in `a`
- But we need to ensure that the total "source" counts match

Wait, let me reframe:

For each value `x`, the number of times `x` appears in `b` must be less than or equal to the number of times `x` or `x-1` appears in `a` (since values can be increased by 1).

But we need an exact match.

Actually, the key insight is:
- For each value `x` in `b`, it comes from either a value `x` in `a` or a value `x-1` in `a`
- So `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`

But we also need to ensure that we don't use more than available elements.

Let me think of it as a constraint system:

Let `c_x` be the number of elements in `a` that were increased to become value `x` in `b`.

Then:
- For each value `x` in `b`: `c_x + freq_a[x] = freq_b[x]`
- Wait, no: if an element had value `x` and was increased by 1, it becomes `x+1`.

So if we define `d_x` as the number of elements that were originally at value `x` and were increased by 1 (so they become `x+1`), then:

- For value `x` in `b`: the total count = elements that were originally at `x` and not increased + elements that were originally at `x-1` and increased = `freq_a[x] - d_x + d_{x-1}`

Wait, this is getting messy.

Simpler approach:

For each value `x` in the range of values, we can say that the number of occurrences of `x` in `b` must be at most the number of occurrences of `x` or `x-1` in `a`.

But more precisely: the total number of "sources" going to value `x` in `b` is at most `freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`, but actually it's exactly the count needed.

Actually, we can just check if for every value `x`, we have:
`freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`

And also, since we can only increase values, we need to ensure that the "demand" doesn't exceed the "supply".

But we also need to ensure that the total number of increases doesn't exceed `n`.

Wait, actually the constraint is simpler: for each value `x`, the number of times `x` appears in `b` must be achievable from the values in `a`.

Specifically, we can get `x` in `b` from:
- Elements that originally had value `x` (unchanged)
- Elements that originally had value `x-1` (increased by 1)

So the total number of elements that can produce value `x` in `b` is `freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`.

We need `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]` for all `x`.

Also, we need to ensure that the total increase is valid.

But there's another constraint: the total increase must be exactly `k`, and we can only increase elements.

Actually, the total number of increases is the sum over all values of the number of elements increased to become higher values.

Let `d_x` = number of elements that were increased from value `x` to `x+1`.

Then `d_x ≥ 0`, and the total increases is `sum(d_x)`.

For each value `x`, the number of elements that end up as `x` is:
- Elements that started at `x` and weren't increased: `freq_a[x] - d_x`
- Elements that started at `x-1` and were increased: `d_{x-1}`

So we have: `freq_a[x] - d_x + d_{x-1} = freq_b[x]`

Rearranging: `d_x = freq_a[x] + d_{x-1} - freq_b[x]`

For this to be valid, we need:
1. `d_x ≥ 0` for all `x`
2. `d_x ≤ freq_a[x]` (can't increase more than available)

But actually, `d_x` represents the number of increases from value `x` to `x+1`, so we need `d_x ≤ freq_a[x]`.

From `d_x = freq_a[x] + d_{x-1} - freq_b[x]`, we get:
- `d_x ≥ 0` ⇒ `freq_a[x] + d_{x-1} - freq_b[x] ≥ 0`
- `d_x ≤ freq_a[x]` ⇒ `freq_a[x] + d_{x-1} - freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x]` ⇒ `d_{x-1} ≤ freq_b[x]`

The first constraint gives us `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + d_{x-1}`

But `d_{x-1}` might be unknown.

Since we're dealing with finite ranges, we can just check the feasibility by starting from the smallest value.

Actually, we can simply check if for each value `x`, `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]` (where `freq_a[-1] = 0`).

This is a standard approach for such problems.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: a = [-1, 1, 0], b = [0, 0, 2]
freq_a: {-1:1, 0:1, 1:1}
freq_b: {0:2, 2:1}

Check:
- For x=0: freq_b[0]=2, freq_a[0]+freq_a[-1]=1+0=1 → 2 ≤ 1? No! This seems wrong.

Wait, I think I made a mistake in the indices.

The constraint should be: for value `x` in `b`, it can come from value `x` in `a` (unchanged) or value `x-1` in `a` (increased by 1).

So `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`

For x=-1: `freq_b[-1]=0`, `freq_a[-1]+freq_a[-2]=1+0=1` → 0 ≤ 1 ✓
For x=0: `freq_b[0]=2`, `freq_a[0]+freq_a[-1]=1+1=2` → 2 ≤ 2 ✓  
For x=1: `freq_b[1]=0`, `freq_a[1]+freq_a[0]=1+1=2` → 0 ≤ 2 ✓
For x=2: `freq_b[2]=1`, `freq_a[2]+freq_a[1]=0+1=1` → 1 ≤ 1 ✓

All constraints satisfied → YES ✓

Example 2: a = [0], b = [2]
freq_a: {0:1}
freq_b: {2:1}

For x=2: `freq_b[2]=1`, `freq_a[2]+freq_a[1]=0+0=0` → 1 ≤ 0? No → NO ✓

Example 3: a = [1,2,3,4,5], b = [1,2,3,4,5]
freq_a: {1:1,2:1,3:1,4:1,5:1}
freq_b: same

For each x: `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`
Since freq_b[x] = 1, freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1] ≥ 1 for all x.
For x=1: 1 ≤ 1 + 0 = 1 ✓
For x=2: 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
For x=3: 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
For x=4: 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
For x=5: 1 ≤ 1 + 1 = 2 ✓

All satisfied → YES ✓

The constraint seems correct.

But is it sufficient? Let me think.

Yes, because if for every value `x` we have `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`, then we can always construct a valid assignment of which elements get increased.

We can greedily assign increases from lower values to higher values, ensuring we don't exceed the available counts.

So the algorithm is:
1. Count frequencies of elements in both arrays
2. For each value `x` in the combined range (min to max of a and b), check if `freq_b[x] ≤ freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]`
3. If all values satisfy this, return "YES", otherwise "NO"

We need to handle negative indices by setting `freq_a[-1] = 0`.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count frequencies
    freq_a = {}
    freq_b = {}
    
    for x in a:
        freq_a[x] = freq_a.get(x, 0) + 1
    
    for x in b:
        freq_b[x] = freq_b.get(x, 0) + 1
    
    # Check constraints for all values in b
    # We need to check for each value x in b: freq_b[x] <= freq_a[x] + freq_a[x-1]
    # where freq_a[x-1] is 0 if x-1 is not in freq_a
    
    # Get all values that appear in b
    values_in_b = set(freq_b.keys())
    
    # We also need to consider negative values, so we'll check from min to max
    min_val = min(min(a), min(b)) if a and b else 0
    max_val = max(max(a), max(b)) if a and b else 0
    
    # Actually, just check all values in b and also values that could be sources
    all_vals = set()
    for x in freq_a:
        all_vals.add(x)
    for x in freq_b:
        all_vals.add(x)
    
    # Check each value in b
    for x in values_in_b:
        # freq_b[x] can come from freq_a[x] (unchanged) or freq_a[x-1] (increased by 1)
        count_from_x = freq_a.get(x, 0)
        count_from_x_minus_1 = freq_a.get(x-1, 0)
        if freq_b[x] > count_from_x + count_from_x_minus_1:
            return "NO"
    
    # Also need to ensure we don't use more elements than available?
    # The constraint should be sufficient as long as we can assign increases
    # But let's verify total elements
    # The total count is preserved (we're just reordering and adding 1s to some)
    # The total elements in b should equal total elements in a, which is n
    # Since we're not removing elements, just adding 1 to some, the total count should be the same
    if len(freq_b) == 0:
        return "YES" if n == 0 else "NO"
    
    # But we should verify the total counts match
    total_a = sum(freq_a.values())
    total_b = sum(freq_b.values())
    
    if total_a != total_b:
        return "NO"
    
    return "YES"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, I see an issue. The total counts must be equal since we're just adding 1 to elements and permuting. So if the frequencies don't sum to the same total, it's impossible.

But in our frequency counting, `sum(freq_a.values()) == n` and `sum(freq_b.values()) == n`, so they should be equal.

So we don't need to explicitly check that.

Let me test with the examples:

Example 2: a=[0], b=[2]
freq_a: {0:1}, freq_b: {2:1}
For x=2: freq_b[2]=1, freq_a[2]+freq_a[1]=0+0=0 → 1 > 0 → "NO" ✓

Example 1: a=[-1,1,0], b=[0,0,2]
freq_a: {-1:1, 0:1, 1:1}, freq_b: {0:2, 2:1}

For x=2: 1 ≤ 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
→ "YES" ✓
